You are an independent quality evaluator, not the producing analyst and not a ruler scorer. [{"question_id": "8B.8", "answer": "The evidence shows selective, reactive adaptation, but not systematic learning or documented replacement of failed implementers. After alleged misuse concerns, the Afghan admission programme was paused, additional data checks and security interviews were introduced, screening capacity was expanded, and the programme restarted; however, the government disputed systemic abuse, only 99 early interviews are reported, admissions remained very low, and NGOs reported continuing delays and implementation problems (BATCH-0011-R03-E013; BATCH-0011-R03-E014; BATCH-0011-R01-E004). BAMF reported revising and regularly updating country-of-origin information in autumn 2023 amid competing NGO and official accounts, but the evidence does not establish that this corrected decision outcomes (BATCH-0011-R04-E003). Other concrete adjustments included mandatory Federal Police records for checks, the enacted repatriate-law amendment responding to court-driven administrative practice, strengthened Federal Network Agency independence and powers, and a proposed narrowing of some police surveillance powers; their enforcement or effects are not established in the supplied record (BATCH-0008-E003; BATCH-0033-E024; BATCH-0033-E025; BATCH-0010-E002; BATCH-0010-E009). Scholz also acknowledged low child-supplement uptake and the need to raise it, without evidence of successful correction (BATCH-0016-E011). Following the Constitutional Court ruling, he accepted the Court's authority and described spending freezes, a proposed reduction of the Climate and Transformation Fund reserve, postponed budget decisions, and proposed legal reauthorization of crisis spending; these measures were immediate or proposed, and final enactment and effects are not shown (BATCH-0016-E016; BATCH-0016-E017; BATCH-0035-E002). Conversely, audits and evaluations continued to identify delayed projects, hesitant decisions, inadequate staffing, underused military schools, ineffective administration, missed digitalization targets, and a residence rule with no positive integration effect, without supplied evidence of completed follow-up, implementer replacement, or improved outcomes (BATCH-0002-E002; BATCH-0003-E009; BATCH-0003-E013; BATCH-0003-E015; BATCH-0009-E012; BATCH-0011-R03-E007; BATCH-0011-R06-E007). In Ukraine, it moved from initial hesitation to substantial, allied-coordinated deliveries of air-defence systems, artillery, armoured vehicles, tanks, training, logistics, and planned winter support, while explicitly balancing Ukrainian defence against avoiding NATO-Russia escalation; the supplied record does not establish that the chosen military objectives were achieved or that escalation was prevented (BATCH-0026-E004; BATCH-0016-E002; BATCH-0016-E005; BATCH-0033-E011). The January Leopard process also shows institutional and coalition resistance: Scholz was still resisting delivery on 19 January, and opponents described the government as delayed, while the governing side characterized coordination as necessary and ultimately defended the planned delivery (BATCH-0031-E001; BATCH-0029-E010). In the Israel-Hamas crisis, Scholz directly pursued hostage release, regional de-escalation, humanitarian access, and protection of Israel through contacts with Israel, Egypt, Türkiye, Qatar, and Jordan; hostages were released during the period and officials credited confidential diplomacy, but the evidence does not establish Scholz’s causal contribution, broader de-escalation, or civilian protection. Germany’s military support for Israel, abstention from a UN ceasefire resolution, and limited public condemnation of reported Israeli violations materially qualify the response (BATCH-0029-E007; BATCH-0031-E002; BATCH-0033-E011; BATCH-0011-R04-E005; BATCH-0008-E016). In Niger, the government supported ECOWAS and African Union mediation, kept diplomatic channels open, treated force as a last resort, and sought restoration of constitutional order, although the outcome and Scholz’s personal role in each decision are not established (BATCH-0032-E008; BATCH-0032-E009). It also organized a concrete Slovenian flood response through THW personnel, equipment, mobile bridges, and €700,000 in financing, though final effectiveness is not reported (BATCH-0032-E008). On migration, the government introduced asylum-procedure reform, independent counselling, strengthened border controls, expanded federal funding, and pursued faster procedures and returns. Yet implementation was delayed, counselling funding was insufficient, border controls were criticized as shifting routes and restricting access, reception capacity was severely strained, and failed removals substantially exceeded effective removals; much of the operational failure lay with Länder and municipalities, limiting direct attribution to Scholz (BATCH-0011-R01-E003; BATCH-0011-R01-E013; BATCH-0011-R04-E013; BATCH-0011-R04-E014; BATCH-0011-R05-E005). Energy measures and gas-price brakes, together with a mild winter, limited economic disruption, but the Constitutional Court ruling invalidated the prior budgetary approach. Scholz accepted the Court’s authority, froze some Climate and Transformation Fund spending, deferred 2024 budget decisions, and proposed a supplementary 2023 budget and emergency authorization for energy relief and flood aid; the supplied period ends before final enactment or implementation, while €60 billion in planned climate and industrial investment was put at risk (BATCH-0016-E016; BATCH-0016-E017; BATCH-0008-E018; BATCH-0024-E013; BATCH-0034-R12-E001). Overall, the record supports active, sometimes coordinated crisis management and partial preservation of objectives, but delays, legal failure, capacity strain, unresolved outcomes, and substantial dependence on allies and subnational actors prevent a consistently effective conclusion. Firefox, Chrome, Edge, Safari.\nYou are here:\nHome\nTime for more efficiency: 2023 Annual Report supplemented by a spring report\nIn December 2023, we presented our 2023 Annual Report. This annual report is now supplemented by a spring report. The spring report contains nine reporting items including examples in which federal financial management was not targeted, inefficient and ineffective.\nThe reporting items in the current sprint report show examples where the Federal Government delays necessary projects and implements parliamentary decisions too hesitantly. We found that the Federal Ministry for Housing, Urban Development and Building delays the establishment of an overarching real estate database, thus jeopardising not only the coordination of federal refurbishment projects but also the timely implementation of the EU Energy Efficiency Directive (No. 35). In the portfolio of the Federal Ministry for Digital and Transport, there is also little targeted action. The Ministry delays the winding up of the Federal Railways Fund despite a decision already taken by the Public Accounts Committee (No. 30).\nIn addition, there is again room for improvement in implementing support programmes: The Federal Ministry for Economic Affairs and Climate Action does not ensure that sufficient staff is available for processing funding applications promptly. As a consequence, the burden for the Federal Office for Economic Affairs and Export Control is too high. This does not only jeopardise target achievement but also the disbursement of funds due to the delayed processing of applications and as a result, the acceptance for support programmes in the population (No. 29).\nTwo reporting items concern the German Armed Forces. The Bundeswehr faces enormous challenges: despite the Bundeswehr Special Fund, it is lacking sufficient budget funds to ensure Germany’s defence capability. At the same time, the Bundeswehr has been running ten technical schools with a continued capacity utilisation of only 50 per cent since 2005, despite our repeated criticism. We recommend again that the Bundeswehr close redundant schools (No. 32). In another case, the Bundeswehr pays a price which exceeds the price limit laid down in the framework agreement of the Federal Government by a factor of 15 for a commercially available battery that runs headsets and microphones for communication support. We urge the Bundeswehr to strictly apply the principle of economy (No. 33).\nThe Government also needs to act on the revenue side to achieve better results. The Federal Finance Ministry imposes itself unnecessary restrictions in digital taxation: The Ministry granted unjustified rights of veto to the economy which render the statutory e-accounting for the taxation of enterprises useless. Instead of terminating this undue exertion of influence, the Ministry intends to introduce a parallel procedure for taxation (No. 28).\nAnnual report prepares decision on granting discharge to Federal Government\nAfter closure of each financial year, the Federal Government is held to account by Parliament and the Federal Council of Constituent States. Following submission, Parliament and the Federal Council of Constituent States decide on granting discharge to the Federal Government for the respective financial year.\nOur annual report provides an important basis for the discharge procedure. The report analyses the Federal Government’s revenue and expenditure, assets and liabilities. The report also provides recommendations on how to address shortcomings and on how to use budget funds in a more efficient and effective manner.\nThe current spring report supplements the annual report by providing nine further reporting items that show where federal financial management needs improvement.\nThe Public Accounts Committee, a sub-committee of German Parliament’s Budget Committee, will deliberate on our annual report during the first six months of 2024. As a rule, the deliberation goes along with the demand addressed to the Federal Government to implement our recommendations.\nYou can download the spring report\nhere\n. Die Karlsruher Richterinnen und Richter hatten am 15. Durch die Verschiebung des Beschlusses gebe es die Zeit, „vorhandene Spielräume auszuloten, Schwerpunkt zu setzen und natürlich auch Ausgaben zu beschränken“, sagte Scholz. Der Bundeskanzler macht deutlich, dass weder bei der Unterstützung der Ukraine, der „Bewältigung der Energiekrise“ noch bei der „Modernisierung des Landes“ nachgelassen werde dürfe. „Es geht für uns alle um viel, es geht um sichere Arbeitsplätze, um eine wettbewerbsfähige Wirtschaft, es geht um ein gutes Leben für die kommenden Generationen und geht um unsere Fähigkeit, auch kommende Krisen zu bewältigen“, sagte Scholz.\nCDU/CSU gegen eine Lockerung der Schuldenbremse\nFür die Unions-Fraktion\ngriff der Fraktionsvorsitzende\nFriedrich Merz (CDU/CSU)\ndie Bundesregierung scharf an. Oktober 2023, 14 Uhr\nOrt:\nBerlin, Paul-Löbe-Haus, Sitzungssaal E 600\nSachverständige aus Wirtschaft, Wissenschaft, Zivilgesellschaft und Kommunalverbänden haben am\nMontag, 9. Oktober 2023,\ndie Forderung bekräftigt, die Digitalisierung der öffentlichen Verwaltung mit Nachdruck voranzutreiben. Dazu sei der vorliegende Entwurf einer Novelle des\nOnlinezugangsgesetzes\n(OZG) (\n20/8093\n(Dokument, öffnet ein neues Fenster)\n) zwar ein „Schritt in die richtige Richtung“, aber nach wie vor unzureichend, hieß es in einer Anhörung im Ausschuss für Inneres und Heimat.\nMit dem 2017 verabschiedeten Onlinezugangsgesetz hatten sich Bund und Länder die Aufgabe gestellt, den Bürgern bis Ende 2022 ein Paket von 575 staatlichen Dienstleistungen auf digitalem Wege zugänglich zu machen. Zum vergangenen Jahreswechsel waren davon indes nur 105 Angebote online geschaltet, mittlerweile sind es einige mehr. Dass das Gesetz sein Ziel bisher verfehlt hat, war Konsens unter den Sachverständigen. Umstritten war, ob die Einführung eines gesetzlichen Rechtsanspruchs auf digitale Verwaltungsleistungen, wie ihn die Unionsfraktion in einem Antrag (\n20/4313\n(Dokument, öffnet ein neues Fenster)\n) fordert, ein geeignetes Mittel der Beschleunigung sei.\nStaatliches Handeln im digitalen Zeitalter\nDer ehemalige Bevollmächtigte des Saarlandes für Innovation und Strategie,\nAmmar Alkassar\n, würdigte in der Anhörung das OZG „trotz aller Defizite“ als bisher umfangreichste Initiative, um staatliches Handeln auf das digitale Zeitalter einzustellen. Die jetzt vorliegende Novelle sei „positiv“, bleibe aber „hinter den Erfordernissen einer raschen Verwaltungsdigitalisierung“ zurück, sagte Alkassem. Es fehlten nicht nur „einige grundlegende Bausteine“, sondern ein „gemeinsames Zielbild“ im Sinne eines „plattformbasierten Ökosystems der öffentlichen Hand“. Zudem sei „Überkomplexität“ abzubauen: „Eine konsequente Umsetzung der Vewaltungsdigitalisierung erfordert einen echten Einschnitt“, mahnte Alkassem.\n„Nach fünf Jahren OZG ist Deutschland nicht dort, wo es in der Verwaltungsdigitalisierung sein sollte“, klagte\nGrudrun Aschenbrenner\nvon\nVorstand der Anstalt für kommunale Datenverarbeitung in Bayern\n\n. Sie wünsche sich „disruptive Änderungen“ und mehr Mut. In anderen Ländern gelte längst das Prinzip, Verwaltungsvorgänge nur noch digital abzuwickeln: „Was hält uns davon ab?“\nStandards, Zielsetzung und Kosten\nAls Vertreterin des\nDeutschen Städtetages\nwies\nUda Bastians\nauf die zunehmende Personalnot der öffentlichen Verwaltung bei wachsenden Anforderungen hin, die die Umstellung auf digitale Verfahren zu einem zwingenden Gebot werden ließen. Bis 2030 werde ein Drittel der derzeit Beschäftigten in den Ruhestand gehen. Zugleich drehe sich das Karussell der Gesetzesnovellen immer schneller. So sei allein das Aufenthaltsrecht seit 2015 nicht weniger als 59 Mal geändert worden: „Die Digitalisierung ist der Schlüssel, um das Land am Laufen zu halten.“ Gemessen daran bleibe der vorliegende Entwurf hinter den Erwartungen zurück. Es fehlten klare Festlegungen auf Standards, Zielsetzung und Kosten.\nFür den\nDeutschen Landkreistag\nwies\nAriane Berger\ndie Forderung nach einem Rechtsanspruch auf digitale Verwaltungsleistungen, der nach Vorstellung der Union 2025 in Kraft treten soll, als untauglich zurück. Die kommunale IT müsse für die Erfordernisse einer weitgehenden Digitalisierung „aufnahmefähig“ gemacht werden. Es bedürfe eines „Standardisierungprogramms“ für die Verwaltung.\nRechtsanspruch auf digitale Verwaltungsleistungen\n„Grundsätzlich begrüßenswert“, aber nicht ausreichend, um das „\nE-Government\n-Entwicklungsland“ in einen „digitalen Staat“ zu verwandeln, nannte\nJonas Botta\nvom\nDeutschen Forschungsinstitut für öffentliche Verwaltung in Speyer\nden vorliegenden Gesetzentwurf und empfahl, ihn „nachzuschärfen“. Die Novelle müsse, wie das bisherige OZG, erneut eine Frist für die Umsetzung enthalten. Es bedürfe klarer Kriterien, um den Realisierungsfortschritt durch eine unabhängige Stelle regelmäßig evaluieren zu lassen. Nicht zuletzt erklärte Botta einen Rechtsanspruch auf digitale Verwaltungsleistungen für sinnvoll.\nAuch\nMarc Danneberg\nvon\nBranchenverband Bitcom\nsah hier eine Möglichkeit, um den „Umsetzungsdruck“ zu erhöhen. Das Gesetz müsse „Planungssicherheit“ gewährleisten sowie Nutzern einen „niedrigschwelligen“ und flexiblen Zugang zu digitalen Verwaltungsleistungen ermöglichen. Es müsse auch unterschiedliche Wege eröffnen, um Zahlungen an Behörden zu leisten.\n\n„International einmaliges Gesetz“\nAnnette Guckelberger, Professorin für Öffentliches Recht an der Universität des Saarlandes,\nerklärte es für „vertretbar“, dass der Entwurf im Unterschied zum bisherigen OZG keine „pauschale Umsetzungsfrist“ enthält. Es müsse aber einen Zeitplan geben und jährliche Fortschrittsberichte, die an den Bundestag zu erstatten seien. Einen Rechtsanspruch halte sie nicht für sinnvoll, sagte Guckelberger. Digitalisierung sei ein komplexer Prozess, das OZG darin nur einer von mehreren Schritten. Auch die Länder seien gefragt, Regelungen zu beschließen.\nDas OZG sei gescheitert, stellte\nProfessor Gerhard Hammerschmid\nvon der\nBerliner Hertie-\nSchool\nfest. Es sei in seiner Zeit zwar ein „international einmaliges Gesetz“ gewesen, aber mit einem so hohen Anspruch, dass es „zum Scheitern verurteilt“ gewesen sei. Hammerschmid sprach sich gegen einen Rechtsanspruch aus, der „administrativ nicht umsetzbar“ sei, dafür umso nachdrücklicher für ein „verbindliches und transparentes“ Monitoring durch unabhängige Experten, zumal, da es künftig keine verpflichtende Umsetzungsfrist mehr geben solle.\nKompetenzen zwischen Bund und Ländern\n„Wir müssen uns gemeinsam auf die zentralen Dinge konzentrieren und das umsetzen, was schnell Nutzen bringt“, mahnte\nAlexander Handschuh\nvom\nDeutschen Städte- und Gemeindebund\n. Er wandte sich gegen die Absicht des Gesetzgebers, mit dem vorliegende Entwurf die Kommunen direkt statt allein der Länder für die Digitalisierung in die Pflicht zu nehmen. Dies schaffe „keinen Mehrwert“, ebenso wenig wie die Forderung nach einem Rechtsanspruch.\nInga Karrer\nvon der\nKoordinationsstelle\nE-Governmen\nt bei der Deutschen Industrie- und Handelsammer\ngab zu bedenken, dass eine erfolgreiche Digitalisierungsstrategie möglicherweise bedeute, die Kompetenzen zwischen Bund und Ländern im Grundgesetz neu zu verteilen. Sie erfordere ein Maß an Zentralisierung, das nach aktueller Verfassungslage nicht zulässig sei.\nSicherheit der Nutzerkonten und der Postfächer\nAls „Teil der digitalen Zivilgesellschaft“, deren Leben mit der Gesetzesnovelle „schneller und einfacher“ werden solle, stellte sich\nBianca Kastl\nvom\n\nBerliner Innovationsverbund Öffentliche Gesundheit\nvor. Sie bemängelte fehlende Klarheit und Transparenz im vorliegenden Entwurf. Dieser können „allenfalls ein Auftaktprozess einer zukunftsfähigen Verwaltung sein, die bei den Menschen ankommt, mehr leider nicht“.\n„Klare gesetzliche Vorgaben für die Sicherheit der Nutzerkonten und der Postfächer“ mahnte der Bundesdatenschutzbeauftragte Ulrich Kelber an. Auch er stellte fest, Deutschland sei „gefährlich unterdigitalisiert“. Jörg Kremer als Vertreter der Föderalen IT-Kooperation, einer in Frankfurt ansässigen Anstalt öffentlichen Rechts, die Bund und Länder bei der Verwaltungsdigitalisierung unterstützen soll, nannte das bestehende OZG „suboptimal“, aber doch zugleich eine „Initialzündung“ für digitalen Wandel. Erforderlich sei, die oftmals zu trägen Entscheidungs- und Abstimmungsprozesse in der öffentlichen Verwaltung der digitalen Welt anzupassen. Malte Spitz von Nationalen Normenkontrollrat warnte, dass auch die OZG-Novelle scheitern könnte, wenn sie nicht nachgebessert werde.\nGesetzentwurf der Bundesregierung\nMit dem Gesetzentwurf sollen die neu geschaffenen Strukturen der Bund-Länder-Zusammenarbeit verstetigt und eine einfache, moderne und digitale Verfahrensabwicklung im übergreifenden Portalverbund ermöglicht werden. Dafür will der Bund unter anderem zentrale Basisdienste bereitstellen und so landeseigene Entwicklungen für das Bürgerkonto und das Postfach ersetzen. Außerdem soll ein schriftformersetzendes qualifiziertes elektronisches Siegel und eine Regelung zu\nDigital-Only\nfür Unternehmensleistungen eingeführt werden.\nDer Nutzen des Gesetzentwurfs besteht aus Sicht der Bundesregierung darin, „dass er den Prozess der Entwicklung nutzerfreundlicher digitaler\nServices\nweiter fördert“. Dies vereinfache das Leben der Menschen und die Tätigkeit von Unternehmen und Selbständigen, soweit diese staatliche Leistungen in Anspruch nähmen.\nAntrag der Union\nDie Unionsfraktion fordert eine gesetzliche Nachfolgeregelung, die die Fortsetzung des auslaufenden Onlinezugangsgesetzes (OZG) sichert. In ihrem Antrag heißt es, es brauche einen rechtlichen, zeitlichen und finanzieller Rahmen für ein OZG 2.0, um den Ländern und Kommunen Planungssicherheit zu geben. Der IT-Planungsrat müsse vor Beginn des formalen Gesetzgebungsverfahren in ausreichender Form beteiligt werden, schreibt die Fraktion weiter. Die Bundesregierung solle zudem einen Rechtsanspruch der Bürgerinnen und Bürger ab dem 1. Januar 2025 auf die im OZG definierten Verwaltungsleistungen des Bundes vorlegen.\nIm Hinblick auf den Umsetzungsrückstand zum Fristablauf des OZG soll die Bundesregierung sich dafür einsetzen, dass „einzelne verfügbare Online-Leistungen auch flächendeckend für alle Nutzerinnen und Nutzer beschleunigt umgesetzt werden“, um eine Zersplitterung des Bereitstellungangebotes zu verhindern, fordert die Fraktion. Über eine gezielte Kommunikationsstrategie in Zusammenarbeit mit allen OZG-Dienstleistern müsse zudem der Bekanntheitsgrad und das Nutzungsverhalten von digitalen Verwaltungsdienstleistungen bei den Bürgerinnen und Bürgern unterstützt werden. (wid/joh/lbr/ste/09.10.2023)\nDokumente\n20/4313 - Antrag: Mehr Tempo bei digitaler Verwaltung - Onlinezugangsgesetz fortführen, Nutzungslücken schließen, Rechtsanspruch einführen\nPDF\n| 161 KB — Status: 08.11.2022\n(Dokument, Link öffnet ein neues Fenster)\n20/8093 - Gesetzentwurf: Entwurf eines Gesetzes zur Änderung des Onlinezugangsgesetzes sowie weiterer Vorschriften zur Digitalisierung der Verwaltung (OZG-Änderungsgesetz - OZGÄndG)\nPDF\n| 1 MB — Status: 23.08.2023\n(Dokument, Link öffnet ein neues Fenster)\nTagesordnung\n51. As Digital Service Coordinator, the BNetzA will also be the central complaints office for citizens and the point of contact for the appointment and coordination of trusted flaggers, or organizations and individuals who are selected to reliably identify content that requires actions.\n5\nIn September 2021, the European Court of Justice (ECJ)—the EU’s supreme court and a part of the Court of Justice of the European Union (CJEU)—upheld a complaint by the EC, ruling that the BNetzA would need to become more independent.\n6\nIn the winter of 2023, the federal government responded to the complaint by strengthening the authority's independence and increasing its powers. The BNetzA is now responsible for determining the conditions and modalities for access to the electricity and gas grids nationwide.\n7\nThe agency's new autonomy, however, has been criticized by the opposition, who argue that it is a “super regulator” without adequate checks and balances.\n8\n1\nMarkus Beckedahl, “Verkehrsministerium gewinnt Fachaufsicht über Bundesnetzagentur”[Ministry of Transport gains supervision over Federal Network Agency],“ Netzpolitik, February 14, 2014,\nhttps://netzpolitik.org/2014/verkehrsministerium-gewinnt-fachaufsicht-u…\n.\n2\nBundesministerium der Justiz und für Verbraucherschutz, “Gesetz über die Bundesnetzagentur für Elektrizität, Gas, Telekommunikation, Post und Eisenbahnen [Law on the Federal Network Agency for Electricity, Gas, Telecommunications, Post and Railways],“ §3, Abs. 3 BEGTPG. July 7, 2005,\nhttps://www.gesetze-im-internet.de/begtpg/index.html#BJNR200900005BJNE0…\n.\n3\nMonopolkommission [Monopolies Commission], “Telekommunikation 2009: Klaren Wettbewerbskurs halten [Telecommunication 2009: stay on target in competition],” Sondergutachten 56, 2009, p. 75,\nhttps://web.archive.org/web/20100821121829/http://www.monopolkommission…\n; European Commission, “Progress Report on the Single European Electronic Communications Market (15th Report),” October 31, 2013,\nhttps://ec.europa.eu/digital-single-market/en/news/progress-report-sing…\n.\n4", "excerpt_sha256": "3054bb3569edd1a119946d0e3b8d252629f6f479e865d76cdc1952fac26a28d6", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": ["The excerpt supports the agency’s criticized political structure, the winter 2023 independence and powers changes, and opposition criticism."]}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0010-E009", "source_id": "SRC-bb0a509f84180c43", "url": "https://freedomhouse.org/country/germany/freedom-net/2024", "title": "Germany: Freedom on the Net 2024 Country Report", "publisher": "Freedom House", "raw_sha256": "311e1b42a7a16fa2c4f17bc1bde5ed207530a183746c600ece7af9f6da1ceab0", "fact_summary": "German surveillance law permits warrant-based source telecommunications monitoring and online searches, while intelligence services have broader powers; in July 2023 the Justice Ministry proposed narrowing police use of remote forensic software and prohibiting secret activation of cameras and microphones, but the proposal had not yet become law.", "question_ids": ["3B.5", "4B.9", "8B.2", "8B.8"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The reform proposal is directly dated July 2023; the underlying surveillance framework predates the period.", "ruler_attribution": "The Justice Ministry in Scholz's federal government proposed the restrictions; the source does not show a personal Scholz directive.", "limitations": ["The proposal's enactment and effectiveness are not established by the document.", "State-level and intelligence-service powers remain broader than the proposed federal police limits."], "start_unit": 65, "end_unit": 68, "locator": "extracted HTML block 65 through extracted HTML block 68", "exact_excerpt": ".\n8\nAndre Meister, “Immer mehr EU-Staaten gegen unverhältnismäßige Chatkontrolle [A growing number of EU countries against disproportionate chat control],“ Netzpolitik, November 23, 2023,\nhttps://netzpolitik.org/2023/internes-protokoll-immer-mehr-eu-staaten-g…\n.\n9\nMarkus Reuter, “EU-Datenschutzausschuss warnt weiterhin vor willkürlicher Überwachung [EU Data Protection Committee continues to warn against arbitrary surveillance],“ Netzpolitik, February 15, 2024,\nhttps://netzpolitik.org/2024/chatkontrolle-eu-datenschutzausschuss-warn…\n; Andre Meister, “EU-Staaten drehen sich bei Chatkontrolle im Kreis [EU countries are going round in circles when it comes to chat control],“ Netzpolitik, April 12, 2024,\nhttps://netzpolitik.org/2024/internes-protokoll-eu-staaten-drehen-sich-…\n.\n10\nAnna Biselli, “Freiwillige Chatkontrolle soll in die Verlängerung [Voluntary chat control to be extended],“ Netzpolitik, January 31, 2024,\nhttps://netzpolitik.org/2024/eu-parlament-freiwillige-chatkontrolle-sol…\n.\nC5\n1.00-6.00 pts\n0-6 pts\nDoes state surveillance of internet activities infringe on users’ right to privacy?\n2.00\n2\n6.00\n6\nArticle 10 of the Basic Law guarantees the privacy of letters, posts, and telecommunications. Furthermore, a groundbreaking 2008 BVerfG ruling established a new fundamental right regarding the “confidentiality and integrity of information technology systems” as part of the general right of personality under Article 2 of the Basic Law.\n1\n\nThese articles generally safeguard offline as well as online communication.\nThe 2008 BVerfG ruling permits covert online searches only in exigent circumstances.\n2\nBased on that ruling, the federal parliament passed a law in 2009 authorizing the BKA to conduct—with a warrant—covert online searches to prevent terrorist attacks and to employ other methods of covert data collection, including the installation of software on a suspect’s computer that intercepts their communications at the source.\n3\nGerman legislation generally distinguishes between two surveillance mechanisms referred to as remote forensic software (Staatstrojaner\n)\n: source telecommunication surveillance (Quellen–TKÜ) and online searches (Online-Durchsuchung). Using source telecommunication surveillance, ongoing communications can be accessed before or after they are encrypted. On the other hand, online searches provide intelligence agencies with the authority to secretly access all files, programs, and messages on a device, including by exploiting security vulnerabilities.\n4\nAmong other tools, German law enforcement and intelligence agencies have deployed Pegasus spyware, which is developed by the Israeli company NSO Group, since 2020.\n5\nThe use of surveillance technologies by authorities is permitted only in relation to certain criminal offenses, with different rules applying to law enforcement and intelligence agencies. Law enforcement agencies have access to both source telecommunication surveillance and online searches, provided that a judicial warrant is obtained. In contrast, intelligence services are generally only allowed to use source telecommunication surveillance, although they are not directly subject to direct oversight. In June 2021, legislative amendments permitted all 19 German intelligence services to utilize an “extended Quellen–TKÜ.”\n6\nThis allows for the monitoring of nonactive communications, including chats and text messages, following the authorization of a surveillance order and it is subject to the rules of source telecommunication surveillance. Consequently, this amendment—developed largely in response to right-wing extremism—has been criticized for blurring the regulatory line between source telecommunication surveillance and online searches.\n7\nArticle 10 of the Basic Law stipulates that limiting the secrecy of letters, posts, and telecommunications may only be ordered on grounds specified in law. These limitations are defined and regulated by the Act for Limiting the Secrecy of Letters, Posts, and Telecommunications (G-10 Act), which was originally enacted in 1968 and has since undergone several amendments. In accordance with the legislation, the Federal Office for the Protection of the Constitution (BfV), the constitutional protection authorities of the federal states, the Military Counterintelligence Service (MAD), and the Federal Intelligence Service (BND) are legally permitted to deviate from Article 10 of the Basic Law under specified circumstances. However, targeted surveillance of individuals is only allowed if there is suspicion of specifically defined criminal offenses, such as treason. The G-10 Commission is tasked with determining whether the implementation of specific surveillance measures is permissible and necessary. Additionally, the commission decides whether the individuals affected must be informed following the termination of the surveillance. The Bundestag is responsible for monitoring the commission’s activities through an elected parliamentary oversight body. At the state level, the respective federal state commissions and parliaments are responsible for decision-making and control. The parliamentary oversight body reports annually on the implementation, type, and scale of the surveillance measures.\n8\nSurveillance conducted by intelligence services under the Act for Limiting the Secrecy of Letters, Posts, and Telecommunications (G-10 Act) has increased slightly. The most recent parliamentary oversight body report, published in 2024, presents data on surveillance under the G-10 Act conducted in 2021. Since 2016, 1,460 surveillance measures on individuals have been authorized, with 243 of these taking place in 2021. A total of 18,849 were targeted under these measures in 2021 compared to 6,897 individuals affected in 2020 and only 1,180 in 2019. The sharp increase is explained by a single BND measure, ongoing since 2018, targeting “Islamist” terrorism.\n9\nBND surveillance measures and their legal basis have been under criticism for several years. A 2016 law explicitly granted the BND permission to monitor domestic internet traffic so long as it targets non–German citizens.\n10\nNevertheless, the law has been scrutinized for its impact on the privacy of German internet users.\n11\nWhile the BND is mainly tasked with foreign intelligence collection, the law permits monitoring of all network traffic channeled through the Frankfurt-located DE–CIX—the world’s largest internet exchange point (IXP)—which would at least unintentionally affect communications by German citizens as well. Furthermore, press freedom groups argued that the law threatens the constitutionally protected work of foreign journalists reporting in Germany and outside the country\n12\n\nand, in January 2018, several nongovernmental organizations (NGOs) and foreign journalists filed a constitutional complaint.\n13\nIn May 2020, the Federal Constitutional Court (BVerfG) declared that the law was unconstitutional in response to the complaint. The BVerfG ruled that the law violated fundamental rights, and the German government's obligation to respect fundamental rights is not limited to German territory. Therefore, the government had to revise the law.\n14\nIn January 2022, an amended version of the BND Law took effect.\n15\nThe law expands the scope of BND activities and allows the interception of up to 30 percent of the transmission capacity of all global telecommunications networks.\n16\nMoreover, the legislation permits the BND to conduct online searches of foreigners abroad in addition to the previously permitted source telecommunication surveillance. While the law protects “individual communications of natural persons,” it allows the BND to collect and process various kinds of information, including inventory, traffic, and content data, enabling the monitoring of communications behavior, financial transaction data, and movement of individuals in Germany and abroad. The insufficient protection of journalists, criticized by the BVerfG, is regulated in an added paragraph. However, the Federal Data Protection Commissioner, opposition politicians, and legal observers have criticized vague exceptions in the protection clause.\n17\nIn January 2023, RSF filed a new complaint to the BVerfG, saying protections for journalists remained too weak.\n18\nHowever, this constitutional complaint was rejected in February 2024, due to insufficient reasoning.\n19\nTelecommunications interception by state authorities for criminal prosecutions is regulated by the criminal code and may only be employed for the prosecution of serious crimes for which specific evidence exists and when other, less intrusive investigative methods are likely to fail. In August 2017, the federal parliament enacted the “Law for More Effective and More Practical Criminal Proceedings.” An extensive list of criminal offenses allows police and investigative authorities to intercept phone calls. The law expands the scope of the list to allow the use of remote forensic software on suspects’ mobile phones, tablets, and computers to enable monitoring of written and spoken text and the copying of data.\n20\nMoreover, the legislation delineates specific criminal offenses that permit the use of source telecommunication surveillance and online searches. The use of remote forensic software for criminal prosecution is regulated by this law, while the law allows the states to regulate preventive hacking in their respective police laws.\n21\nIn line with legislation, several state laws have been enacted that grant police vastly expanded powers to access communications.\n22\nCritics consider the 2017 legislation unconstitutional due to its expansive scope and long list of applicable offenses, claiming it contradicts the 2008 BVerfG ruling on Article 2 of the Basic Law.\n23\nAt the national level, the Society for Civil Rights (GFF) filed a constitutional complaint against the law in 2018.\n24\nIn April 2023, GFF was given the opportunity to make representations in the proceedings.\n\n25\nNevertheless, the BVerfG had not reached a verdict as of the end of the coverage period.\n26\nThe constitutional complaint against the police law in Baden-Württemberg was rejected in 2021; in its reasoning, the BVerfG derived a state duty to protect the information technology (IT) security of all Germans. Therefore, the state must weigh the exploitation of security flaws in its deployment of remote forensic software against the information security interests of the public.\n27\nIn 2022, 4,981 telecommunication interceptions were ordered, most frequently for drug crimes.\n28\nIn addition, the surveillance of IT devices by means of the remote forensic software and source telecommunications surveillance, was ordered in 94 proceedings and carried out in 49 cases.\n29\nFinally, six online searches were ordered, of which four were actually carried out.\n30\nIn comparison, in 2021, 5,174 telecommunication interceptions, 23 instances of source telecommunications surveillance and 9 online searches were conducted.\n31\nIn July 2023, the Ministry of Justice presented a bill to place some restrictions on the use of remote forensic software for police authorities. Currently, 44 different crimes permit the use of source telecommunication surveillance and 33 particularly serious crimes permit the use of online searches. With the amendment, the 33 crimes will apply to both the use of source telecommunication surveillance and online searches. In addition, the secret activation of cameras and microphones will be explicitly prohibited in the amendment.\n32\nIn Hesse in 2018, legislation establishing law enforcement’s right to use remote forensic software also allowed agencies to conduct automated analysis of personal data. Hesse uses Gotham, a predictive policing tool produced by the US–based company Palantir to analyze different data sources, including internal police databases and Facebook data.\n33\nNorth Rhine–Westphalia purchased similar software in 2020, Bavaria in 2022, and Hamburg reportedly planned to implement it in 2019.\n34\nThese tools have been criticized for using discriminatory data bases and producing mass errors.\n35\nIn February 2023, the BVerfG ruled that bills in Hesse and Hamburg that legalized data mining and predictive policing were unconstitutional.\n36\nFollowing the ruling, most federal states’ ministries have declared their intention to refrain from utilizing Gotham or analogous software in the future. Nevertheless, Bavaria, North Rhine–Westphalia, and Hesse have indicated that they intend to continue using the software, while Berlin, Hamburg, and Thuringia were evaluating its potential deployment as of the end of the coverage period.\n37", "excerpt_sha256": "2a04c3c282216dc175d0f103c3e4c8096c6f26c8019af266ab34469c9790ea21", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": ["The excerpt supports warrant-based law-enforcement surveillance, broader intelligence-service powers, the July 2023 bill, and its uncompleted status."]}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0011-R01-E003", "source_id": "SRC-a007cee33f0d9205", "url": "https://asylumineurope.org/wp-content/uploads/2024/06/AIDA-DE_2023-Update.pdf", "title": "Country Report: Germany; 2023 Update", "publisher": "ECRE / AIDA", "raw_sha256": "35a16fff9b7b255d989c2a5cc6e56b8de7d67ce65e07a782935d6cac97244469", "fact_summary": "The asylum-procedure reform that entered into force on 1 January 2023 introduced independent counselling, changed interview and appeal rules, and aligned procedural time limits more closely with EU law; however, implementation was delayed, NGO access to arrival centres was restricted by a March 2023 court ruling, and funding for independent counselling was insufficient and lacked a reliable long-term strategy.", "question_ids": ["3B.4", "3B.8", "4B.4", "6B.2", "6B.5", "8B.2", "8B.6", "8B.8"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The reform and its reported implementation problems concern 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The reform was enacted by the coalition government; the source attributes the access restriction to the Federal Administrative Court and does not establish Scholz’s personal involvement.", "limitations": ["The report does not quantify the effect of counselling on decisions or legal outcomes."], "start_unit": 16, "end_unit": 17, "locator": "page 16 through page 17", "exact_excerpt": "significantly, with 239,614 cases as of 31 December 2023. The overall recognition rate at first instance\n     stood at 68.6% (i.e., 21.6% refugee status, 36.2% subsidiary protection and 10.9% humanitarian\n     protection). The reform\n  entered into force on 1 January 2023. The most important changes of the reform include:\n       o The introduction of independent counselling for asylum seekers, instead of the state-run\n          counselling which was introduced in 2019 (seeInformation for asylum seekers and access to\n          NGOs and UNHCR)\n       o Changes to the rules for personal interviews: an additional ground for dispensing with the\n          interview was introduced when the BAMF is of the opinion that the foreigner is unable to\n          attend a hearing due to permanent circumstances beyond their control and the possibility of\n          conducting video interviews (see Personal interview)\n       o The provisions on time limits for the asylum procedure were changed in order to closely mirror\n          the relevant provisions of the EU Asylum Procedure Directive (see Regular procedure -\n          General (scope, time limits))\n       o A change in the rules for onward appeals (Revision) to the Federal Administrative Court,\n          according to which the latter can now also adjudicate on the facts of the case, rather than\n          merely on points of law (see Regular procedure - Onward appeal).\n       o Complete revision of the grounds for cessation of the residence permit and substantial\n          amendments to the grounds of revocation of the residence permit (see Cessation and review\n          of protection status).\n  In contrast to the rules introduced by the legal reform concerning independent counselling, the\n  Highest Administrative Court decided in March 2023 that local authorities are not required to provide\n  regular access for NGOs and welfare associations to reception centres.15 Only where independent\n  counselling is explicitly requested by the applicant, must access be granted. The repercussions of\n  this and whether this leads to more difficulties in accessing legal aid need to be examined in practice.\n  In general, the introduction of independent counselling falls short of its goals due to delays in\n  implementation, practical obstacles for NGOs to access the arrival centres, insufficient funding for\n  2023 and 2024 and lack of reliable long-term funding strategies from the state (see Information for\n  asylum seekers and access to NGOs and UNHCR).\n\nv Continued reading out of mobile devices in asylum procedures: The Act to Improve Removals\n  introduced a new norm that allows the authorities to read out mobile devices. Legal scholars criticise\n  the provision as unconstitutional and the Federal Administrative Court has already emphasised that\n  it must be applied only ultima ratio (see Lodging the application).\n\nv    Increased border controls and suspected illegal push backs: The Federal Government has\n     introduced further ‘temporary’ stationary border controls on the borders with Poland, the Czech\n     Republic and Switzerland during 2023. They have regularly been prolonged and are still in place.\n     With these increased border controls, a significant decrease in asylum claims on the border has been\n\n\n\n15\n       Highest Administrative Court (BVerwG), Decision 1 C 40.21, 28 March 2023, available in German at:\n       https://bit.ly/480lN0o, para. 27f.\n\n\n                                                     16\n\nobserved while the number of people arriving is further increasing. Some stakeholders suspect illegal\n     push backs by the Federal Police (see Access to the territory and push backs).\n\nv    Breaches of church asylum: In light of a hardening political climate, the authorities have breached\n     into church premises where persons were housed in the context of church asylum in 2023. Due to\n     strong protests, they however did ultimately not transfer the asylum seekers concerned (see Dublin\n     - Procedure).\n\nv Humanitarian admission programme for Afghan nationals: On 17 October 2022, the Federal\n  Government launched a federal admissions programme which had been announced in the coalition\n  agreement of 2021.16 Following false accusations of misuse, the admission programme was put on\n  hold in March 2023. It was resumed in June 2023, but a new screening mechanism was introduced\n  which includes data checks and specific security interviews (see Differential treatment of specific\n  nationalities in the procedure). NGOs criticise that the temporary suspension and the introduction of\n  the additional screening procedure caused further delays and that the programme does not live up to\n  the originally envisaged admission scheme.\n\nv Unaccompanied minors:\n     o Due to overburdening of local authorities in 2023, unaccompanied children often did not have\n        access to special care such as psychological assistance and to schooling (see Legal\n        representation of unaccompanied minors)\n     o Age assessment has been repeatedly criticised for providing a large margin of discretion to\n        the authorities (Identification – Age assessment of unaccompanied children).\n\nv Safe Country concept: In 2023, Georgia and Moldova were added to the list of safe countries of\n  origin. NGOs criticise this decision. They claim that, in these countries, minorities such as Roma and\n  LGBTIQ+ members face discrimination and that there has been a backlash to democracy and the\n  rule of law (see Safe country of origin).\n\nv    Removal ban for Iran: The federal removal ban for Iran has been lifted as of January 1st, 2024.\n     However, over the course of 2023, four persons were removed to Iran despite the ban.\n\nv Key jurisprudence on subsequent applications: The CJEU decided in February 2024 that its\n  rulings can qualify as ‘new elements’ under the APD.17 This means that subsequent applications\n  should be declared admissible if CJEU court rulings strongly suggest the existence of a ‘new legal\n  situation’ even if the CJEU ruling only concerns the interpretation of EU law. The ruling came as a\n  result of a preliminary ruling requested by the Administrative Court of Sigmaringen in 2022, asking\n  whether the two conditions for the admissibility of a subsequent application are compatible with the\n  EU Asylum Procedures Directive (APD).18 In addition, the CJEU ruled that a temporary return to the\n  country of origin has no impact on further applications as to whether these further applications are\n  subsequent applications19 (see Subsequent applications).\n\nReception conditions\n\nv Reintroduction of emergency shelters: Due to the increasing arrivals of protection seekers,\n  several municipalities, especially larger cities, have had to reintroduce emergency shelters. Without\n  any change in 2023, especially in larger cities, exhibition grounds and/or made-up tent facilities are\n  used to host asylum seekers. The conditions in these emergency shelters are below standards.\n\n16\n      For further details, see AIDA, Country Report Germany - Update on the year 2021, April 2022, available at\n      https://bit.ly/3XnN7RS, 11.\n17\n      Ibid.\n18\n      Art. 33(2)d and 40(2) recast APD. The reform\n  entered into force on 1 January 2023. The most important changes of the reform include:\n       o The introduction of independent counselling for asylum seekers, instead of the state-run\n          counselling which was introduced in 2019 (seeInformation for asylum seekers and access to\n          NGOs and UNHCR)\n       o Changes to the rules for personal interviews: an additional ground for dispensing with the\n          interview was introduced when the BAMF is of the opinion that the foreigner is unable to\n          attend a hearing due to permanent circumstances beyond their control and the possibility of\n          conducting video interviews (see Personal interview)\n       o The provisions on time limits for the asylum procedure were changed in order to closely mirror\n          the relevant provisions of the EU Asylum Procedure Directive (see Regular procedure -\n          General (scope, time limits))\n       o A change in the rules for onward appeals (Revision) to the Federal Administrative Court,\n          according to which the latter can now also adjudicate on the facts of the case, rather than\n          merely on points of law (see Regular procedure - Onward appeal).\n       o Complete revision of the grounds for cessation of the residence permit and substantial\n          amendments to the grounds of revocation of the residence permit (see Cessation and review\n          of protection status).\n  In contrast to the rules introduced by the legal reform concerning independent counselling, the\n  Highest Administrative Court decided in March 2023 that local authorities are not required to provide\n  regular access for NGOs and welfare associations to reception centres.15 Only where independent\n  counselling is explicitly requested by the applicant, must access be granted. The repercussions of\n  this and whether this leads to more difficulties in accessing legal aid need to be examined in practice.\n  In general, the introduction of independent counselling falls short of its goals due to delays in\n  implementation, practical obstacles for NGOs to access the arrival centres, insufficient funding for\n  2023 and 2024 and lack of reliable long-term funding strategies from the state (see Information for\n  asylum seekers and access to NGOs and UNHCR).\n\nv Continued reading out of mobile devices in asylum procedures: The Act to Improve Removals\n  introduced a new norm that allows the authorities to read out mobile devices. Legal scholars criticise\n  the provision as unconstitutional and the Federal Administrative Court has already emphasised that\n  it must be applied only ultima ratio (see Lodging the application).\n\nv    Increased border controls and suspected illegal push backs: The Federal Government has\n     introduced further ‘temporary’ stationary border controls on the borders with Poland, the Czech\n     Republic and Switzerland during 2023. The ruling came as a\n  result of a preliminary ruling requested by the Administrative Court of Sigmaringen in 2022, asking\n  whether the two conditions for the admissibility of a subsequent application are compatible with the\n  EU Asylum Procedures Directive (APD).18 In addition, the CJEU ruled that a temporary return to the\n  country of origin has no impact on further applications as to whether these further applications are\n  subsequent applications19 (see Subsequent applications).\n\nReception conditions\n\nv Reintroduction of emergency shelters: Due to the increasing arrivals of protection seekers,\n  several municipalities, especially larger cities, have had to reintroduce emergency shelters. On 16 October\n2023, controls were also introduced at the border with Poland, the Czech Republic and Switzerland and\nwere extended again in December 2023 and March 2024.61 The agreement with Switzerland also includes\ncontrols on Swiss territory. This allows the authorities to prevent people from entering German territory\nand they are then consequently not responsible for any asylum requests.62 The prolongations occurred\ndespite a ruling of the CJEU of 26 April 2022 in which the court states that border controls cannot exceed\na duration of 6 months unless there is a new threat justifying a renewed introduction of controls for another\nsix months maximum.63 The extension has been continually criticised by NGOs such as PRO ASYL, who\nargue that controls lead to refusals of entry of would-be asylum seekers in Germany, who are denied\naccess to an assessment by the BAMF of whether Germany might be responsible for handling their\nasylum application.64 A representative of the union of police officers repeatedly criticised the extensions,\non the grounds that they do not reduce irregular immigration but rather shift routes to other land borders.65\n\nIn 2018, following a heated political debate, a new procedure was introduced which enables the Federal\nPolice to refuse entry at the border and send persons back to Greece and Spain within 48 hours if they\nhave previously applied for asylum there.66 This procedure is based on administrative regulations and\nspecial administrative readmission agreements with the two countries. These returns are therefore not\nbased on the Dublin Regulation, but on a refusal of entry under the (national) notion of ‘safe third countries’\nin combination with administrative arrangements concluded with other EU Member States. Since 2019, it\nwas only applied to persons found at the Austrian-German border, as this was the only border where\ncontrols continue to take place. While being heavily debated in 2018, the introduction of the new\nprocedure had little effect in practice: between August 2018 and May 2021, only 50 persons were returned\n(46 returns to Greece and 4 to Spain) on the basis of the readmission agreements with these countries.67\nWhile no refusals of entry were carried out between May 2021 and the end of 2021 according to the\nFederal Police,68 two persons were returned to Spain each year in 2022 and 2023 and none to Greece.69\nTherefore, the political debate over the return procedures at the border, which had even triggered a\ngovernment crisis in 2018, has been described as ‘absurd’ in retrospect.70\n\n\n\n\n60\n      Ibid, 8.\n61\n      Dw.de, Innenministerin Faeser verlängert Grenzüberwachung, 8 December 2023, available in German at:\n      https://bit.ly/48iodYA; European Commission, ‘Temporary Reintroduction of Border Control’, available at:\n      https://bit.ly/3HLwSGd.\n62\n      Federal Government, Reply to parliamentary question by The Left, 20/8274, 07 September 2023, available in\n      German at: https://bit.ly/49GtKZX, 21.\n63\n      CJEU. Special procedural guarantees\n\n                                    Indicators: Special Procedural Guarantees\n\n      1. Are there special procedural arrangements/guarantees for vulnerable people?\n                                                           Yes        For certain categories      No\n             v If for certain categories, specify which: Unaccompanied children, traumatised persons\n                                                         Victims of torture or violence\n\n         2.1. Adequate support during the interview\n\nThe BAMF does not have specialised units dealing with vulnerable groups. According to the BAMF, all\ncase workers complete the EUAA training module on ‘Interviewing Vulnerable Persons’.576 If an applicant\nor a Federal State authority submits information to the BAMF that indicates vulnerability (such as medical\nrecords or information about specific physical, mental, intellectual or sensory impairments), such\ninformation is transferred to the case worker in charge who can decide to take measures such as allocate\nmore time for the interview, appoint an interpreter of a specific gender or allow the person to bring a\ntrusted person of their choice to the interview.577\n\nFor specific groups of vulnerable persons, the BAMF employs ‘special officers’ (Sonderbeauftragte)\nresponsible for interviews and decisions on claims by applicants with special needs. Special officers also\nadvise their colleagues in dealing with vulnerable applicants and are contact persons for specialised\ncounselling services and psycho-social centres.578 Staff members who become special officers must\ncomplete a training module for the specialisation they want to achieve. In addition, they follow the EASO\ntraining modules for their specialisation.579 Training covers both the identification and in the treatment of\nvulnerable persons.580 According to the BAMF, continuous training is offered for specific topics in the realm\nof the special officers’ responsibilities.581 The BAMF guidelines stipulate that the following cases shall be\nhandled in a particularly sensitive manner and, if necessary, by specially-trained decision-makers:582\n     v Unaccompanied children;\n     v Victims of gender-specific prosecution;\n     v Victims of human trafficking; and\n     v Victims of torture and traumatised asylum seekers.\n\nAs of June 2023, a total of 1,267 BAMF had one or more roles as special officers. This corresponds to\nroughly a third of full-time equivalent positions allocated to the first instance procedure (see Number of\n\n574\n        Nerea González Méndez de Vigo, Alterseinschätzung – ein Irrgarten ohne Ausweg? The distribution among areas of responsibilities was the\nfollowing: Unaccompanied children (410), victims of gender-specific persecution (312) traumatised\npersons and victims of torture (291), victims of trafficking (254).583\n\nSpecially trained case officers may be included at all times of the asylum procedure, or take over, also\nprior to the interview, if vulnerabilitites are known. For example, if it becomes evident during the interview\nthat an asylum seeker belongs to one of these groups, the officer conducting the interview is obliged to\nconsult a special officer, in addition to notifying the reception centre if necessary and authorised by the\napplicant.584 A note on how the officers are planning to proceed must be added by the special officer to\nthe file, particularly if the special officer takes over the case as a result of their consultation. According to\ninformation provided by the government, there is an obligation in cases of unaccompanied minors for\nspecial officers to take over responsibility for the asylum procedures. In other cases of other vulnerable\ngroups, the specially trained case officer must be consulted and there are two options for further\nprocedures: either the special officer adopts an advisory role or they take over responsibility for the\nprocedure.585 However, the BAMF does not record the number of cases in which special officers are\nconsulted or in which procedures are handed over to special officers.586\n\nLawyers have reported that the introduction of special officers has led to some improvement in the\nhandling of ‘sensitive’ cases. The special officers receive special training in the area of LGBTIQ, they\nconduct interviews upon consultation by other BAMF officers and serve as multipliers for their\ncolleagues.587However, there have also been examples of cases in which indications of trauma and even\nexplicit references to torture did not lead to special officers being consulted.588 It has further been criticised\nthat there are shorcomings in the effective implementation of procedural guarantees for LGBTIQ+\npersons, which increases the risk of false decisions.589 There is no individual right to have a special officer\nhandling a person’s case, except for unaccompanied minors for whom this is mandatory. But if evidence\nsuggests that the person is vulnerable or if the person claims to have certain vulnerabilities, the interviewer\nis required to involve a special officer in the procedure, e.g. as consultant.590 However, the Administrative\nCourt of Berlin ruled that if special vulnerabilities have been detected, the absence of a special officer in\nthe asylum procedure constitutes a violation of procedural rights of vulnerable asylum seekers.591\n\n        2.2. Exemption from special procedures\n\nThe German Asylum Act exempts neither unaccompanied children nor persons with special procedural\nguarantees from the airport procedure, despite an express obligation under the recast APD to provide for\nsuch exemptions under certain conditions.592 It also makes no reference to ‘adequate support’ which\nshould be provided to those requiring special procedural guarantees.593\n\n\n\n\n583\n       The government notes that the figures cannot be added since some officers may have qualified in more than\n       one area; Federal Government, Reply to parliamentary question by The Left, 20/7503, 28 June 2023, available\n       in German at: https://bit.ly/3Sb12cF, 2-4.\n584\n       BAMF, Die Identifizierung vulnerabler Personen im Asylverfahren. Reference source not\nfound.Subsequent applications). The number of ‘upgrade appeal’ cases and decisions remains high,\nhowever, likely as a result of long court procedures. Between January and the end of May 2023, courts\ndecided on 5.736 such appeals, and in 806 cases (14%) granted asylum or refugee protection, while in\n4,930 cases (86%) the appeal did not lead to an improvement in the protection status.753 9,525 such\nappeals of Syrian nationals were pending as of 31 May 2023, a similarly high number to the end of 2022\n(9,458).754\n\nThe removal ban for Syria that had been in place since 2012 expired at the end of December 2020. The\nban was based on a common decision of the Federal States and the Federal government, but could not\nbe renewed due to disagreement regarding the possibility to remove criminals and ‘persons posing a risk’\nrelated to terrorist activities (‘Gefährder’).This was heavily criticised by NGOs and organisations such as\nthe German Institute for Human Rights, UNHCR and Caritas.755 The removal statistics for the first half of\n2023 indicate that 410 removals of Syrian nationals took place.756 However, Syria is not listed as a country\nof destination for removals in the first half of 2023, meaning that the removals of Syrian nationals took\nplace to other countries, for example to other EU Member States in the form of Dublin transfers or\nremovals following a refusal of entry.757 As of February 2023, the Federal Government declared that it\ncurrently sees no possibilities for removals to Syria.758\n\n      2. Afghanistan\n\nEmergency evacuation since the Taliban takeover in 2021\n\nWith the takeover of the Taliban on 15 August 2021, the German government started an evacuation\noperation for German nationals in Afghanistan as well as Afghan nationals who had worked for German\nauthorities, the military and ‘especially endangered persons’. Between 16 and 26 August 2021, a total of\n5,300 persons were evacuated, out of which 4,400 Afghan nationals. The evacuated persons entered\nGermany via an emergency visa (based on Section 14 and 22 Residence Act).759 Upon arrival, the BAMF\nthen examined whether persons had already been granted permission for an admission from abroad\n(Section 22 Residence Act). If this was not the case, and if the Federal Ministry decided no such\npermission could be granted, persons were informed of this and of the possibility to apply for asylum in\nGermany. 760\n\nAfter the end of the evacuation, German authorities continued to receive and examine notifications of risk\nof former employees and of “especially endangered individuals” in exceptional circumstances. Sub-\ncontractors and consultants who worked for German authorities only indirectly are considered on a case\nby case basis according to the Federal Government. 761 If the examination confirms that the persons are\nat risk due to their work for a German authority, admission permissions continue to be granted according\n\n753\n       Federal Government, Response to parliamentary question by The Left, 20/5709, 17 February 2023, available\n       in German at: https://bit.ly/3K3w3MX, 43.\n754\n       Federal Government, Response to parliamentary question by The Left, 20/8222, 5 September 2023, available\n       in German at: https://bit.ly/3SklJCR and 20/5709, 17 February 2023, available in German at:\n       https://bit.ly/3K3w3MX, 41.\n755\n       FRA (European Union Fundamental Rights and Community.762 Permissions are also included for close family\nmembers (spouses and minor siblings), other relatives are only considered in hardship cases.763 Since\ntravelling out of Afghanistan is difficult and costly, the Federal Government has provided 32 million Euros\nin funding to the GiZ (the German Development Agency) to support persons in leaving the country. It has\nbeen criticised that despite the acknowledgement that fleeing Afghanistan is difficult, there is no\nsystematic evacuation scheme.764 As a result, there have been reports that persons who managed to\ndepart from Afghanistan have been pushed back by Bulgarian, Turkish and Greek border police.765\nPersons admitted to Germany mainly leave via Pakistan and Iran. 766 Germany has also concluded an\nagreement with the government of Pakistan to allow the concerned persons to enter Pakistan with a\nTazkira K (ID card) instead of a passport as required by the Taliban to leave the country. However, in mid-\nSeptember 2022 the Federal Government reported that a total of 34 former employees and family\nmembers are known to have died in Afghanistan.767\n\nAdmission schemes\n\nGermany has been operating an admission scheme for local staff of German ministries in Afghanistan\nsince 2013. The scheme is based on Art. 22 (2) Residence Act (Temporary residence permission to uphold\nthe political interest of the Federal Republic of Germany). The eligibility criteria depend on the status of\nthe former employee. Only former staff (and their close family members) directly employed by German\nentities are covered by the programme.768\n\nFrom the takeover of the Taliban in 2021 to 17 October 2022, according to the Federal Government,\n38,100 persons had been issued a permission for admission to Germany (out of which 24,500 were former\nemployees and eligible family members, and 13,600 were especially vulnerable persons and their eligible\nfamily members). Around 26,000 of these (68.2%) persons had entered Germany up until that time. As of\n10 December 2021, a total of 28,053 permissions for admission from abroad had been issued to Afghan\nnationals and 8,014 persons had entered Germany as of the same date.769 The admission scheme for\nlocal staff continues in parallel to the new humanitarian admission scheme announced on 17 October\n2022.\n\nOn 17 October 2022, the Federal Government launched an additional federal admissions programme\nwhich had been announced in the coalition agreement of 2021.770 The government describes the\nprogramme and procedure as follows: the programme is geared towards persons who ‘have exposed\nthemselves to particular risk through their commitment to women’s and human rights or their work in the\nspheres of justice, politics, the media, education, culture, sport or academia and are thus vulnerable’ or\n‘due to the special circumstances of their individual cases have experienced or are experiencing violence\nor persecution based on their gender, sexual orientation or gender identity or religion and are therefore at\nconcrete and personal risk. In particular, these are victims of serious individual women’s rights violations,\nhomo- or transphobic human rights violations or vulnerable representatives of religious\n\n\n\n\n762\n      Federal Ministry of the Interior and Community, Reply to written question by Canan Bayram (The Greens),\n      20/5046, available in German at: https://bit.ly/3NvUljF, 81-121.\n763\n      Federal Government, Reply to parliamentary question by The Left, 20/3430, 15 September 2022, available in\n      German at: https://bit.ly/477WMjm, 11.\n764\n      Taz.de, Die Mauern werden höher, 27 February 2023, available in German at: https://bit.ly/48d59Ln.\n765\n      Ibid.\n766\n      Federal Government, Reply to parliamentary question by The Left, 20/3430, 15 September 2022, available in\n      German at: https://bit.ly/477WMjm, 2.\n767\n      Federal Government, Reply to parliamentary question by The Left, 20/3430, 15 September 2022, available in\n      German at: https://bit.ly/477WMjm, 16.\n768\n      ECRE, Afghans Seeking Protection in Europe, December 2021, available at: https://bit.ly/3krGfED, 11.\n769\n      Deutscher Bundestag, parliamentary question by The Left, 20/791, 22 February 2022, available in German at:\n      https://bit.ly/3RPrRUC, 1.\n770\n      AIDA, Country Report Germany - Update on the year 2021, April 2022, available at: https://bit.ly/3XnN7RS, 11.\n\n\n                                                      122\n\ngroups/communities.’771 The admission programme includes family members of those persons, which\nincludes spouses or same sex partners, minor children and other family members who can prove a\nrelation of dependency (beyond economic dependency) with the main person and find themselves in a\nsituation of concrete and lasting danger due to the work or vulnerability of the main person.772 The German\ngovernment appoints agencies (including civil society organisations) who can put forward names of\nsuitable persons, who must still be living in Afghanistan, via an IT application containing a questionnaire\nof a total of 41 pages.773 The names of these organisations are not made public by the government, but\naccording to a press report, PRO ASYL, Reporters without Borders, Mission Lifeline and Luftbrücke Kabul\nare taking part in the programme as of 20 December 2022.774\n\nThe Government then takes the admission decision based on selection criteria that include vulnerability\n(in line with the UNHCR catalogue of criteria), relation to Germany e. g. through language skills, family\nties, previous stays or work for German authorities or projects, level of personal exposure of the person\ne. g. through a visible / exposed position or public statements, and a special political interest on the side\nof Germany to admit a person.775 As with the previous admission programme, selected persons first\nreceive assistance to leave Afghanistan and enter a neighbouring country and are then issued a visa and\ntravel assistance by the German embassy in that country. Persons who enter Germany under the\nprogramme receive a residence permit for three years. The Federal State responsible for reception of the\npersons is to be determined according to the quota system for the distribution of asylum seekers (see\nRegistration of the asylum application), although family ties and other ‘criteria supporting integration’ are\nto be taken into account.776\n\nWhen announcing the programme, the Federal Government declared that ‘the new programme is now to\nbe implemented quickly’ and that it planned to approve around 1,000 requests per month, which is about\nthe amount of permissions granted in the months preceding the announcement. The programme is\nplanned to run until the end of the current government’s term in 2025.777 As of 30 June 2023, 229 persons\nhave been selected for admission.778 As of October 2023, only 13 of them had been admitted, due to the\npause of the admission procedure (see below).779 According to a press report, the NGOs Mission Lifeline\nand Luftbrücke Kabul alone have received around 32,000 requests as of early November 2022.780\n\nIn March 2023, the Federal Foreign office declared that all admission programs would be put to a halt for\nan indefinite time due to alleged abuse. The German newspaper Cicer and Bild published that, according\nto information they received, the admission programs had been used to bring radical Islamists to\n\n\n\n771\n      Federal Foreign Office, Joint press release by the Federal Foreign Office and the Federal Ministry of the\n      Interior and Community and Community on the federal admission programme for people from Afghanistan\n      who are at particular risk, 17 October 2022, available at: http://bit.ly/3J47ZJA.\n772\n      Federal Ministry of the Interior and Community and Federal Foreign Office, FAQs on the humanitarian federal\n      admission programme for Afghanistan, available at: http://bit.ly/3iVp3Xx.\n773\n      rbb.de, Aufnahmeprogramm für Afghanen startet schleppend, 20 December 2022, available in German at:\n      http://bit.ly/3iVdBva.\n774\n      rbb.de, Aufnahmeprogramm für Afghanen startet schleppend, 20 December 2022, available in German at:\n      http://bit.ly/3iVdBva.\n775\n      Federal Ministry of the Interior and Community and Federal Foreign Office, FAQs on the humanitarian federal\n      admission programme for Afghanistan, available at: http://bit.ly/3iVp3Xx.\n776\n      Federal Ministry of the Interior and Community and Federal Foreign Office, Anordnung des\n      Bundesministeriums des Innern und für Heimat gemäß § 23 Absatz 2, Absatz 3 i. A speaker of the\nForeigner’s Office declared that there were no evidence supporting a systemic misuse of the admission\nprograms.782 Only in one case a person who applied for admission has been identified as a possible\n‘threat’ (‘Gefährder’). Nevertheless, the Foreigners Office along with the Minister of Interior decided to\nintroduce additional screening mechanisms and to halt the admission program until the screening\nmechanism is in place.783 The screening procedure involves an automatic data reconciliation with\ndatabases from the national security agencies and interviews with the German domestic intelligence\nservice (Bundesamt für Verfassungsschutz), Federal Criminal Police Office (Bundeskriminalamt), the\nFederal Police or BAMF officers acting on behalf of these national security services.784 The admission\nprograms were restarted from 26 June 2023. In the first months of the newly introduced screening\nprocedure (26 June – 21 July 2023) 99 screening interviews took place, no security concerns have been\nidentified among those.785 The Federal government further states, that the capacities for the screening\nmechanism have been expanded since its start. Accordingly, as of August 2023 several hundreds of\nscreenings can be conducted per month.786\n\nThe Left party and NGOs such as PRO ASYL welcomed the launch of the programme but criticised that\n1,000 admissions per month was too low given the ‘real pressure of persecution’ for ‘people who have\nfought for democracy and human rights’. PRO ASYL further criticised that the relatively abstract selection\ncriteria could lead to an ‘ethically highly ambivalent protection lottery’787 especially in connection with the\nfact that only authorised agencies could put forward people and that the application is through an\nalgorithm-based IT application with little room to put forward specific individual circumstances.788 The\nNGO Kabul Luftbrücke reported problems with the IT application in October and November, leading to\ndelays in sending the online forms to authorities. A further point of critique is that the programme does not\nextend to persons who have managed to flee Afghanistan.789 Several NGOs also voiced concerns over\nthe practical implementation, demanding a better staffing of the counselling and coordination centres for\nthe programme and questioning the ‘organization and content’ of the procedure,790 especially given that\nit is required to have a passport in order to leave the country while obtaining one is made extremely\ndifficult by the Taliban government. One year after the official launch, in October 2023, several NGOs\nreflected on the development of the program. They demanded that the originally envisaged number of\n1,000 admissions per months should be fulfilled, that the procedure should be more transparent and that\nin additional admission schemes are necessary to meet the needs for protection.791\n\nIn addition to the Federal Government, several Federal States (Thuringia, Berlin, Hessen and Bremen)\nhave implemented admission programmes based on family ties to Afghans living in the respective Federal\nStates (for more information see Family Reunification). "raw_sha256": "35a16fff9b7b255d989c2a5cc6e56b8de7d67ce65e07a782935d6cac97244469", "fact_summary": "Between 2022 and May 2023, only 55 of 2,500 applications from Russian men eligible for military service had been decided, and only 11 were decided positively in the first half of 2023; civil-society organizations attributed the low recognition rate partly to outdated country-of-origin information, while BAMF said its reports were revised in autumn 2023 and regularly updated.", "question_ids": ["3B.4", "6B.2", "6B.5", "8B.8"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The quantitative decisions cover 2022-May 2023 and the positive decisions cover the first half of 2023; the information-update response occurred in autumn 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The reported decisions and information-management response concern BAMF and the Federal Ministry of the Interior; no personal Scholz instruction is shown.", "limitations": ["The source presents competing NGO and BAMF assessments and does not independently resolve whether the recognition rate was erroneous."], "start_unit": 127, "end_unit": 128, "locator": "page 127 through page 128", "exact_excerpt": "2,851 first-time applicants.813 The overall protection rate (share of positive decisions when formal\ndecisions are not considered) was 29.0% in 2023, up from 24.0% in 2022 and 15.5% in 2021.814\n\nAccording to NGO PRO ASYL, the main obstacle for Russian nationals seeking protection in Germany is\nthe lack of legal escape routes, as no flights from Russia to Germany are available and as countries along\nthe EU’s external border no longer allow Russian citizens to enter with Schengen visas.815 Germany has\nonly ‘granted humanitarian visas in a few exceptional cases of people who have made public\nappearances, such as critical journalists’ according to PRO ASYL, while ‘German embassies and\nconsulates generally reject such applications’.816 PRO ASYL reports that in some cases, German\nembassies in countries other than Russia accept long-term visa applications from Russian nationals (e.g.,\nfor work, study or family reunification) for persons ‘who would be unreasonably endangered if they were\nto return to the responsible mission in Russia to apply. This may be the case for human rights defenders,\njournalists, dissidents and conscientious objectors.’\n\nDeserters of the Russian army – those who flee from active military service – can be granted refugee\nstatus as they are threatened with persecution on political grounds, according to the Federal Ministry of\nthe Interior and Community,817 while more restrictive criteria apply to conscientious objectors. According\nto established jurisprudence, refusal to enter military service is, as such, not a ground for granting asylum.\nConscientious objectors can only be granted refugee status in cases where the punishment for refusal to\nperform military service is disproportionately high, if the refusal triggers political persecution, or ‘if the\nasylum seeker would have been obliged to participate in war crimes, crimes against peace or crimes\nagainst humanity during military service and refuses military service for this reason’.818 The BAMF decides\non these applications on an individual basis. As of 18 February 2023, there were only two known BAMF\ndecisions on applications from Russian nationals fleeing military service. In one of them, the person was\ngranted protection but based on political activities. The other case concerned a person over the age of 40\nand without prior military training, and the BAMF assumed that it was not sufficiently likely he would be\nforced to participate in the war. The decision was criticised by civil society organisations, who argue that\nthe Russian recruiting practice is broader and more unpredictable than what was assumed by the\nBAMF.819 Whereas the number of asylum applications from men eligible for military service continued to\nrise in 2023, according to several answers to requests from The Left to the Minister of Interior the\nrecognition rate of men eligible for military services is still very low. Accordingly, only 55 out of 2,500\napplications from men eligible for military services have been decided between 2022 and May 2023.820\nOnly 11 of those have been decided positively in the first half of 2023.821 According to Pro Asyl, the low\nrecognition rate by the BAMF partly stems from outdated country of origin information on the prosecution\nof deserters and those who object to military service.822 The BAMF rejects this view and states that\navailable reports on military service have been revised in autumn 2023 and are regularly updated.\n\n813\n       BAMF, Antrags-, Entscheidungs- und Bestandsstatistik (01-12/23), 8 January 2024, available in German at:\n      https://bit.ly/3UjFWf0;
